2. Key Technical Concepts:
- SuperCollider GUI: Window, CompositeView, Button, Slider, Knob, PopUpMenu, NumberBox, LevelIndicator, TextView, EZRanger, StaticText
- `s.waitForBoot` vs `s.doWhenBooted` — the latter doesn't trigger an extra boot (fixed double-boot warning)
- `s.options.inDevice` / `s.options.outDevice` — separate in/out device selection (macOS supports this; assistant was initially wrong about "one device only" and corrected this)
- `s.options.numInputBusChannels` / `s.options.numOutputBusChannels` — set before reboot to open the right number of channels
- `s.options.sampleRate` — nil = default (hardware rate), or explicit integer (44100, 48000, etc.)
- `ServerOptions.inDevices` / `ServerOptions.outDevices` — lists available audio devices
- `RecordBuf.ar(SoundIn.ar(inCh), bufnum, loop:0, doneAction:2)` — record into a buffer
- `Buffer.readChannel(s, path, channels:[0], action:{...})` — load audio file; `action:` fires when buffer is truly ready (async)
- `BufDur.kr(bufnum).reciprocal` — scan pointer frequency that makes Warp1 loop in exactly one buffer-length
- `LFSaw.ar(freq).range(0,1)` — sawtooth scan pointer for Warp1
- `SendReply.kr(Impulse.kr(20), '/inAmp', Amplitude.kr(SoundIn.ar(inCh), 0.01, 0.2))` — ships audio amplitude to the language at 20Hz for metering
- `OSCdef(\name, {|msg| ...}, '/path')` — named listener (named so re-running doesn't stack duplicates); `msg[3]` = the amplitude value from SendReply
- `.defer` — bounces GUI work onto the GUI thread from other threads
- `.fork` — runs a background sequence (used for the write+sync+reload path after recording)
- `Platform.defaultTempDir +/+ "marrsRecA.wav"` — temp file path for recording trim
- `Main.elapsedTime` — seconds since SC started; used to measure recording length
- `valueAction_(n)` — sets a widget's value AND runs its action (used for auto-jump: `typeMenuA.valueAction_(jumpMenuA.value)` + `startButtonA.valueAction_(1)`)
- `Select.ar(which, [signals])` — switchable DSP inside a SynthDef
- Parallel arrays: `imageSynthDefsA` + `imageSynthTrigA` — same-index arrays for the switchable image-synth system
- `imageSynthTrigA` — booleans: `true` = triggered synth (fresh Synth per pixel), `false` = held (create-once then .set)
- `AppClock` — used for scan Routines so GUI slider values can be read safely inside the loop
- `Buffer.alloc(s, s.sampleRate * 30, 1)` — 30s mono scratch buffer for recording

**Key sections inside the workstation:**

*loadFileA — reusable audio loader (shared by dialog AND recording):*
```supercollider
loadFileA = {|path, onDone|
    soundFileA = SoundFile.new;
    soundFileA.openRead(path);
    bufferA = Buffer.readChannel(s, path, channels: [0], action: { {onDone.value}.defer });
    soundFileViewA.soundfile_(soundFileA);
    soundFileViewA.read(0, soundFileA.numFrames);
    selectionSpecA = ControlSpec(0, soundFileA.numFrames);
    soundFileViewA.setSelection(0, selectionSpecA.map([0.1, 0.2]));
    soundFileViewA.mouseUpAction.value(soundFileViewA);
};
```

*Rec button stop branch (recording trim + reload):*
```supercollider
var recFrames = ((Main.elapsedTime - recStartTimeA)*s.sampleRate).asInteger.min(bufferA.numFrames);
var path = Platform.defaultTempDir +/+ "marrsRecA.wav";
var oldBuf = bufferA;
recSynthA.free;
log.("recorded " ++ (Main.elapsedTime - recStartTimeA).round(0.01) ++ "s");
{
    oldBuf.write(path, "wav", "int16", recFrames);
    s.sync;
    oldBuf.free;
    {
        loadFileA.(path, {
            typeMenuA.valueAction_(jumpMenuA.value);
            startButtonA.valueAction_(1);
        });
    }.defer;
}.fork;
```


4. Errors and Fixes:
- **Double "already booting" warnings**: `s.waitForBoot` inside `buildMarrs` was triggering a second boot after the button already did `s.reboot`. Fixed by changing to `s.doWhenBooted` which just waits — no boot call.
- **Warp playing at wrong length**: LFSaw hardcoded at `1/15` (15s period) ignored actual buffer length. Fixed with `BufDur.kr(bufnum).reciprocal`.
- **PlayBuf playing ~10% fast**: slider default `0.7` mapped to `1.1×` speed via `linlin(0,1,-1,2)`. Fixed with `value_(2/3)`.
- **Recording always 30s long, no tight loop**: RecordBuf fills the whole pre-allocated 30s buffer even for short takes. Fixed: measure elapsed time → compute recFrames → `oldBuf.write(..., recFrames)` to disk → reload via `loadFileA` → buffer becomes exact recording length.
- **TGrains broken on recordings**: `soundFileA.duration` was nil/stale for recordings (no SoundFile object created). Fixed: `centerPosInSecondsA` now reads `bufferA.duration` instead.
- **Cmd+W quit/boot race**: `win.onClose` was calling both `masterMeter.remove` and server-level cleanup in bad order. Fixed: guarded `masterMeter` removal with `if(masterMeter.notNil)`, added QUIT ENGINE button for orderly shutdown (stop routine → remove meter → `s.quit` → `win.close`), `onClose` no longer quits the server.
- **`\imageRGBA` RGB spread lost**: User had wrapped SinOsc in `Mix.ar(...)` before Pan2, collapsing 3 tones to mono. Removed the `Mix.ar` wrapper to restore L/C/R panning. Then `.sum` on line 1300 correctly mixes the 3 panned pairs to stereo.
- **OSCdef receiving nil**: `msg[3]` is the amplitude — if using the wrong index the meter would show nil. Correct index is 3 for a basic `SendReply`.

5. Problem Solving:
- Established the `loadFileA` reusable loader pattern — extracted from the dialog's inline code so both file-open and recording share one load path. The `onDone` hook fires from `Buffer.readChannel`'s `action:` callback, guaranteeing the buffer is ready before auto-playing.
- Established triggered vs held synth pattern for image synths: `imageSynthTrigA` boolean array marks which SynthDefs spawn-per-pixel (true) vs create-once-then-.set (false).
- Established the "knobs live on triggered synths" pattern: since triggered synths aren't tracked in `synthAStart`, the scan must read knob values fresh inside `args` per-pixel rather than relying on `.set`.
- Boot menu design: single block holding `buildMarrs` + the setup window as locals — no globals. `buildMarrs.value` called from the Boot button after `s.reboot`.

8. Current Work:
The most recent exchange was about converting the `ins` and `outs` NumberBoxes in the audio setup/boot menu into PopUpMenus that automatically reflect the correct number of channels. The user asked:

> "for the choice of ins and outs, I want to make the number box into a pop up box which then automatically has the correct number of in/outs"

The boot menu currently declares:
```supercollider
var buildMarrs, audioSelectWin, devIn, devOut, ins, outs, srMenu;
```

With `ins` and `outs` as NumberBoxes (`.value_(2)` each). The Boot button reads them with `.value.asInteger`. The challenge is that SuperCollider has no pre-boot API to query a device's actual channel count — so "automatically correct" means either a curated common-counts list (1,2,4,6,8,10,12,16,18,22,24,32,64), or dynamically updating when a device is selected (using a lookup table). The simplest SC-compatible approach is a PopUpMenu with common channel counts, and optionally updating it when `devIn`/`devOut` changes based on a known map of device names → channel counts.

This gives the user the "automatically correct" feel they asked for, with a sensible fallback list.

[CLAUDE]
Honest heads-up first, because it shapes the answer: **SuperCollider can't actually ask a device "how many channels do you have?" before it boots** — there's no API for it. (That's different from the input-channel meter chooser, which worked because it read `numInputBusChannels` *after* boot, once the count was already decided.) So a dropdown that truly auto-detects the device's count isn't directly possible.

But there are two good ways to get most of what you want:

**Option A — a dropdown of channel-count choices** (pick from a list instead of typing). Swap each `NumberBox` for a `PopUpMenu`:

```supercollider
ins = PopUpMenu(audioSelectWin, Rect(10, 152, 90, 26))
.items_(["1", "2", "4", "6", "8", "16", "18", "22", "24", "32"]);

outs = PopUpMenu(audioSelectWin, Rect(140, 152, 90, 26))
.items_(["1", "2", "4", "6", "8", "16", "18", "22", "24", "32"]);
```

Then in the Boot button, change `.value.asInteger` to **`.item.asInteger`** for both (a PopUpMenu's `.value` is the index, but `.item` is the chosen string — `"18"` → `18`):

```supercollider
s.options.numInputBusChannels  = ins.item.asInteger;
s.options.numOutputBusChannels = outs.item.asInteger;
```

That gets you tidy dropdowns and no typos — but *you* still pick the number; it isn't device-aware.

**Option B — auto-fill for your own gear.** Since SC can't discover the counts, but *you* know them (MOTU = 18/22, built-in = 2/2), we add a tiny lookup so that picking a device sets the channel menus for you. When `devIn`/`devOut` change, it looks the name up in a little table and sets the dropdown. You fill that table once with your devices' numbers, and from then on it *feels* automatic.

So: **A** is the quick, honest version (dropdowns, you choose). **B** layers the "it fills itself in when I pick my MOTU" behaviour on top, at the cost of one small hand-written table.

Which do you want — just the dropdowns (A), or the auto-fill-by-device (B)?

Still not *discovering* the counts from the hardware — you'd type them in once — but it *feels* automatic from then on.

[CLAUDE]
Good. Two things: an output bus selector under the fader, and a level meter beside it. Let me explain what we're building before we start typing anything.

---

**Output selector** — each synth already has an `\out` argument that defaults to `0` (bus 0, the first hardware output pair). We'll add a PopUpMenu below the fader that lists stereo pairs — "Out 1-2", "Out 3-4", etc. — and wires into `.set(\out, ...)` live, and also passes the right output when a new synth starts.

**Level meter** — we can't read a hardware output bus from the language directly. But in SC, if you add a *monitoring synth* to the tail of the server's node tree, it runs *after* the main synth has written, and can use `In.ar` to read what's on that output bus. That monitor synth sends amplitude via `SendReply`, we catch it with an `OSCdef`, and it updates a `LevelIndicator` sitting beside the fader. Same pattern as the input meter you already have.

---

**Step 1 — declare the new variables.**

Find line 222:
```supercollider

```

```supercollider

```

`outSelA` = the output PopUpMenu, `outLevelMeterA` = the LevelIndicator widget, `outLevelMonA` = the tail-synth that monitors the bus level.

Type that in, and tell me when it's done.

[CLAUDE]
**Step 2 — add the monitoring SynthDef.**

Let me find where the other SynthDefs live so you know exactly where to put this.

[CLAUDE]
Find line 1611 — the `OSCdef(\inMeter, ...)` line. We're adding two things directly after it (before the effects section that starts with `//effects here`).

Type these two blocks after line 1611:

```supercollider
SynthDef(\outLevelMonA, { |outBus = 0|
    var sig = In.ar(outBus, 2);
    SendReply.kr(Impulse.kr(20), '/outAmpA',
        Amplitude.kr(sig.sum, 0.01, 0.1), 0);
}).add;

OSCdef(\outMeterA, {|msg| { outLevelMeterA.value = msg[3] }.defer }, '/outAmpA');
```

What's happening here: `In.ar(outBus, 2)` reads two channels (stereo) from whichever hardware output bus we're monitoring. `sig.sum` collapses the stereo pair to mono so we can measure a single amplitude value. `SendReply` fires 20 times a second with that level, and the `OSCdef` catches it and pushes it into the LevelIndicator widget on the GUI thread.

This SynthDef will run at the *tail* of the server's node tree, meaning it runs after the main synths have already written their audio to the bus — so it reads the real output signal.

Type that in and let me know when it's done.

**Step 3 — add the LevelIndicator widget next to the fader.**

The amp fader (`ampSliderA`) sits at `Rect(10, 10, 30, 300)` on the masterWin — it's 30px wide, 300px tall, starting at x=10. We'll place a thin level indicator immediately to its right, at x=45, same height.

Find where `ampSliderA` is created (around line 726). Look for the closing of that block — it ends with `layout:'vert'` and a closing `);`. Right after that closing `);`, add:

```supercollider
outLevelMeterA = LevelIndicator(masterWin, Rect(45, 10, 12, 300))
.drawsPeak_(true)
.warning_(0.7)
.critical_(0.9);
```

`drawsPeak_(true)` gives you the little peak-hold marker at the top. `.warning_` and `.critical_` are thresholds — at 70% of full scale the bar goes yellow, at 90% it goes red. Values are 0.0–1.0 because that's how amplitude comes back from `Amplitude.kr`.

Tell me when that's in.

[CLAUDE]
**Step 4 — add the output selector PopUpMenu.**

This goes right after the `outLevelMeterA` line you just typed. Add:

```supercollider
outSelA = PopUpMenu(masterWin, Rect(10, 315, 47, 22))
.items_(
    (0..((s.options.numOutputBusChannels / 2).asInteger - 1))
    .collect({|i| "Out " ++ (i*2+1) ++ "-" ++ (i*2+2)})
)
.action_({|menu|
    if(synthAStart.notNil, { synthAStart.set(\out, menu.value * 2) });
    outLevelMonA.set(\outBus, menu.value * 2);
});
```

A few things worth understanding here:

The items list is built dynamically from however many output channels your device was booted with. `s.options.numOutputBusChannels / 2` gives you the number of stereo pairs — so if you booted with 6 outputs you get `["Out 1-2", "Out 3-4", "Out 5-6"]`. The `* 2` in the action converts the menu index back to a bus number (index 0 = bus 0, index 1 = bus 2, index 2 = bus 4, etc.).

When the menu changes, it does two things: moves the running synth to the new output, and moves the monitoring synth to read the same bus.

Tell me when that's in.

The `addAction: \addToTail` is the important bit — it places this synth at the very end of the server's processing order, so it runs after every other synth has already written audio to the bus. Without that, it might run before the main synth and read silence.

[USER]
Oh. ok. lets make a new view thats kind of like a sequencer. I want to use Latch.ar /Stepper.kr etc and have some kind of gui that controls that.

[CLAUDE]
Great idea. Before we start building, a couple of questions to make sure we build the right thing.

`Stepper.kr` counts through steps on a trigger — so it's the heartbeat of a classic step sequencer. `Latch.ar` sample-and-holds a signal when triggered — useful for snapping a modulation source to a new value each step. They fit together nicely.

The main design question is: **what does the sequencer control?**

Two directions:

**Option A — a standalone sequencer player.** A new entry in the typeMenuA ("Seq" or similar). Each step sets a pitch and/or amplitude. Plays a tone (SinOsc, Pluck, etc.) on each step. Classic melodic step sequencer.

**Option B — a modulation sequencer.** Not a new player, but a separate panel that steps through values and feeds them into whichever player is running — e.g. stepping through grain positions in the buffer, or stepping grain rate, or amplitude. More interesting with granular synthesis because you're sculpting *texture* in steps rather than notes.

The second one is more unusual and fits the granular aesthetic better — imagine a 16-step sequence that snaps the playhead to different positions in your buffer, creating a chopped, rhythmic version of whatever you've loaded.

What's your instinct — melodic note sequencer, buffer position/parameter stepper, or something else entirely?

[CLAUDE]
Take your time — it's a good one to sit with. Come back when you've got a direction and we'll build it.

[USER]
hey. can you explain to my why my synthdef only triggers once?

heres the code. I'm kinda copying the first function into a Synthdef

(
{
	SinOsc.ar(
		Latch.kr(
			LFSaw.kr(MouseX.kr(0.1,20),0, 500, 600),
			Impulse.kr(10)
		),0, 0.3
	)
}.scope
)

(
SynthDef(\aLatch, {
	arg out=0, freq=400, trigSpeed=10;
	var sig, env, trig;
	// env = EnvGen.kr(Env.linen(0.01,0.5, 0.1), doneAction:2);
	trig = Impulse.kr(trigSpeed);
	sig = Latch.ar(LFSaw.kr(1,mul: 400, add: 500), trig);
	sig = Blip.ar(sig, 4, 0.2);
	Out.ar(out, sig)
	
}).add
)

[CLAUDE]
Good question — there are actually two things going on here.

**First: the envelope fires once and kills the synth.**

The commented-out line is `EnvGen.kr(Env.linen(...), doneAction:2)`. When `doneAction:2` is there, the envelope fires *once when the synth starts*, plays through, and then `doneAction:2` frees the synth. So you hear one note, then silence. The Latch and Impulse are still running inside the synth — but the synth gets freed after one envelope cycle.

The fix is to **pass the `trig` signal into `EnvGen` as the gate argument** instead of leaving it to fire once at birth. When EnvGen receives a trigger (a short 1→0 pulse), it *restarts the envelope from scratch*. So every time your Impulse fires, a new note envelope starts. The synth stays alive and keeps retriggering.

**Second: `Latch.ar` vs `Latch.kr`.**

In your SynthDef, both the LFSaw and the Impulse are control-rate signals (`.kr`). But you're using `Latch.ar`. Using the audio-rate version with control-rate inputs can cause unreliable behaviour. Since you're just generating a frequency value (not audio), use `Latch.kr`.

So the corrected version looks like this conceptually:

```supercollider
trig = Impulse.kr(trigSpeed);
env = EnvGen.kr(Env.linen(0.01, 0.5, 0.1), trig);  // retriggers on each impulse
sig = Latch.kr(LFSaw.kr(1, mul: 400, add: 500), trig);
sig = Blip.ar(sig, 4, 0.2) * env;  // multiply by env to shape each note
```

The key change is that `trig` goes into both `Latch.kr` AND `EnvGen.kr` — the same Impulse that grabs a new pitch value also starts a fresh envelope. No `doneAction:2` because you don't want the synth to free itself — you want it to keep stepping.

Give that a try and let me know what you hear.

[USER]
ok. 

a = Synth(\aLatchB)
a.set([\trigSpeed, 2])

why does nothing happen why I fire a.set?

[CLAUDE]
The square brackets — that's the issue.

`a.set` takes arguments as pairs directly, not wrapped in an array:

```supercollider
a.set(\trigSpeed, 2)
```

What you wrote — `a.set([\trigSpeed, 2])` — passes a single array as one argument. SC doesn't know what to do with that and silently ignores it.

Remove the brackets and it'll work.

[USER]
I'm such an idiot

[CLAUDE]
You're not — that's a really easy one to get wrong because in lots of other contexts in SC you *do* pass arrays. The inconsistency is SC's fault, not yours.

.states_([
	["Off", Color.black, Color.red],
	["On", Color.black, Color.green],
	
])
.action_({|btn|
	if(btn.value==1){
		synthGo=Synth(\aLatchB)
	}{
		synthGo.set(\gate,0)
		
	}
});

win.front;

SynthDef(\aLatchB, {
	arg out=0, freqA=400,freqB=500, sawSpeed=1,trigSpeed=10, nHarm=1, gate=1;
	var sig, env1,env2, trig;
	trig = Impulse.kr(trigSpeed);
	env1 = EnvGen.kr(Env.linen(0.01,0.5, 0.1),trig);
	env2 = EnvGen.kr(Env.linen(0.01,0.5, 0.1),gate);

	sig = Latch.kr(LFSaw.kr(sawSpeed,mul: freqA, add: freqB), trig*env1);
	sig = Blip.ar(sig, nHarm, 0.2);
	Out.ar(out, sig)*env2;
	
}).add;



);
)

[CLAUDE]
Two issues.

**The main one — `Out.ar` is in the wrong place.**

This line:
```supercollider
Out.ar(out, sig)*env2;
```

`Out.ar` is a terminal UGen — it writes `sig` to the bus and its return value is meaningless. Multiplying *after* it does nothing. The audio has already been sent. You need to multiply `sig` by `env2` *before* it goes into `Out.ar`:

```supercollider
Out.ar(out, sig * env2);
```

**The second one — `Env.linen` doesn't respond to gate going to 0.**

`Env.linen` is designed to be *triggered* — it fires once when it receives a pulse and plays through attack-sustain-release. It doesn't hold while gate is 1 and release when gate drops to 0. So even if the `Out.ar` was right, setting `\gate, 0` wouldn't gracefully release the sound.

For a proper open/close gate, use `Env.asr` instead:

```supercollider
env2 = EnvGen.kr(Env.asr(0.01, 1, 0.1), gate, doneAction: 2);
```

`asr` means attack-sustain-release. It holds at full level while `gate=1`, and when `gate` drops to `0` it triggers the release and then `doneAction:2` frees the synth cleanly.

Fix both of those and your button should work.

[CLAUDE]
Nice — the code is looking solid. Before noting two small things about the current code, let me answer your design question.

All three of those — switchable oscillator, switchable input, switchable trigger — use the exact same pattern you already know from the main workstation: `Select.ar` or `Select.kr`. You build all the signal options at once in the SynthDef, then `Select` just picks which one to output based on an index argument. A PopUpMenu in the GUI sends the index with `.set`.

For example, switchable trigger source would look like:
```supercollider
arg trigType = 0;
var trig = Select.kr(trigType, [
    Impulse.kr(trigSpeed),   // 0 = regular clock
    Dust.kr(trigSpeed),      // 1 = random timing
    LFPulse.kr(trigSpeed)    // 2 = pulse wave
]);
```

Same idea for the oscillator and the Latch input signal.

Two small things I noticed in your current code before you build further:

1. `win.front` is missing — the window won't appear without it
2. The block isn't closed — you're missing a final `)` at the very end

Worth fixing those first so the base runs cleanly. Then which of the three do you want to tackle first — trigger, input signal, or oscillator?

Good call. These are based on the SynthDef's own default values, mapped back into the 0–1 slider range:

- `latchTrigSpeedA` range is 0.5–20 Hz. Default `trigSpeed=10` maps to `(10-0.5)/(20-0.5)` ≈ **0.49**
- `latchFreqAA` range is 50–400. Default `latchFreqA=200` maps to `(200-50)/(400-50)` ≈ **0.43**
- `latchFreqBA` range is 50–2000. Default `latchFreqB=500` maps to `(500-50)/(2000-50)` ≈ **0.23**
- `latchSpeedA` range is 0.1–20. Default `patSpeed=1` maps to `(1-0.1)/(20-0.1)` ≈ **0.05**

Hey. I passed the granularWorkstation 1_5 over to a freind and they had an error saying that it didnt recognize "extension" and so no recordings could be made. What was that?

[CLAUDE]
That rings a bell — let me look at the recording code in V0_1_5 to confirm what's happening.

[CLAUDE]
Found it. Line 2474:

```supercollider
if(path.extension !="wav"){path = path ++ ".wav"};
```

The problem is that `path` coming out of `Dialog.savePanel` is a plain **String**, and `.extension` is a method on the `PathName` class — not on String. So SC throws a doesNotUnderstand error on `.extension`, which your friend's SC paraphrased as "didn't recognize extension."

The fix is to wrap `path` in `PathName(...)` before calling `.extension`:

```supercollider
if(PathName(path).extension != "wav"){ path = path ++ ".wav" };
```

`PathName` is SC's class for parsing file paths — it knows how to pull out the extension, the directory, the filename without extension, etc. A raw String doesn't have any of that.

One line change, tell your friend to swap it in.

[USER]

**There are a few bugs in the SynthDef that need fixing before anything else:**

1. **The `Select` arrays use `;` instead of `,` as separators.** In SC, `[a; b; c]` doesn't make an array — the semicolons make each line a separate statement and only the last value survives. Both your `patType` and `oscType` arrays have this.

2. **`oscType` is not declared as an arg.** It's used in `Select.ar(oscType, [...])` but missing from the arg list on line 1718.

3. **`Saw.ar()` has no arguments** — it needs the latched frequency: `Saw.ar(sig, 0.2)`.

4. **`env2` still has a 1-second attack** — `Env.asr(1,1,1)`. The `1` means the sound fades in over a full second every time you press start.

```supercollider
Dust.kr(trigSpeed),
```

[USER]
ok. before we go any firther. how difficult is it to make switchable views depending on the signal oscillator? for instance, Pulse has a width parameter I'd like to use, but Blip has nHarms, and Saw has non.

[CLAUDE]
Not very difficult at all — and you've actually already done the hard version of this with the image synths. The pattern is exactly the same.

The idea is: inside `latchSynthViewA`, you make a small sub-view (or just individual widgets) for each oscillator's unique parameters. The osc PopUpMenu's `.action_` shows the right one and hides the others. For example:

- Osc menu → "Saw" selected: hide pulseWidthView, hide nHarmsSlider
- Osc menu → "Pulse" selected: show pulseWidthView, hide nHarmsSlider
- Osc menu → "Blip" selected: hide pulseWidthView, show nHarmsSlider

Since Pulse and Blip each only have one extra parameter, you don't even need full sub-views — just two sliders that you show and hide individually. Then in the SynthDef, `pulseWidth` and `nHarms` are always arguments (with sensible defaults), and the `Select.ar` passes the right one to the right oscillator.

The only slightly fiddly part is that `Blip.ar` takes frequency and number of harmonics as separate arguments, whereas `Saw.ar` and `Pulse.ar` just take frequency and amplitude. So each branch of the `Select.ar` wires up its own parameters:

```supercollider
Select.ar(oscType, [
    SinOsc.ar(sig, 0, 0.2) * env2,
    LFTri.ar(sig, 0, 0.2) * env2,
    Saw.ar(sig, 0.2) * env2,
    Pulse.ar(sig, pulseWidth, 0.2) * env2,
    Blip.ar(sig, nHarms, 0.2) * env2
])
```

Shall we build this into the SynthDef and the view now, before moving on to the PopUpMenus?

[USER]


`pulseWidth=0.5` defaults to a square wave (50% duty cycle). `nHarms=4` gives Blip a reasonable starting harmonic count.

[USER]
done. Can you tell me why I feel like theres a bug in my patSpeed slider. it only really works if I move that slider before any others. once I move the trigger speed it no longer works??

[CLAUDE]
Found it. Look at the trigSpeed slider action:

```supercollider
synthAStart.set(\trigSpeed, sl.value.linlin(0, 1, 0.5, 20).asInteger.postln)
```

That `.asInteger` is the culprit. When the slider is at a low position, `linlin` gives something like `0.5`, and `.asInteger` rounds it down to `0`. `Impulse.kr(0)` fires exactly once at synth startup and never again — so the trigger dies, Latch.kr never updates, and the pitch freezes. Moving patSpeed changes the LFSaw but since Latch is frozen it has no audible effect.

`Impulse.kr` handles fractional rates perfectly well — 0.5 Hz means one trigger every two seconds. Remove the `.asInteger`:

```supercollider
synthAStart.set(\trigSpeed, sl.value.linlin(0, 1, 0.5, 20))
```

While you're there — also check your patSpeed slider range. It currently reads `linlin(0, 1, 50, 40)` which maps slider left→50, slider right→40. That's both inverted and a very narrow band. It should probably be something like `linlin(0, 1, 0.1, 20)` to give you a useful sweep from slow to fast.

[USER]
ok. good.. but now theres a loud bang when changing the osc type

[CLAUDE]
Classic SC problem. `Select.ar` does a hard instantaneous switch — it jumps to the new signal at whatever sample value it happens to be at that moment, which causes a sudden discontinuity in the waveform. Your ears hear that as a bang.

The fix is two things working together: `SelectX.ar` instead of `Select.ar`, and `Lag.kr` on the index. `SelectX.ar` crossfades between adjacent signals based on a *float* index (so 1.5 = halfway between items 1 and 2). `Lag.kr` smoothly ramps the integer step from the old index to the new one over a short time, so instead of jumping instantly from 0 to 2 it glides through 0→0.5→1→1.5→2 over about 50ms. `SelectX` crossfades during that ramp.

In the SynthDef, find:
```supercollider
sig = Select.ar(oscType, [
```

Change it to:
```supercollider
sig = SelectX.ar(Lag.kr(oscType, 0.05), [
```

That's the only change needed. The `0.05` is 50 milliseconds — fast enough to feel instant but slow enough to avoid the click. You can make it shorter if it feels sluggish.

One small side effect: switching from Sine (0) to Saw (2) briefly passes through Tri (1) for those 50ms. Usually inaudible, but worth knowing.

Two different things here — one's a real bug, one's the nature of sample-and-hold.

## The amp slider — real bug

Line 809, that `.max(0.1)` is the leftover amp guard you told me to drop. It clamps the floor at 0.1 so the fader physically can't reach zero:

```supercollider
5, {synthAStart.set(\latchAmp, sl.hi)};
```

## The pat speed "breaking" on noise — not a code bug

This one's subtler and worth understanding, because it's inherent to how your sequencer is wired rather than a typo.

Walk through what happens. Your pattern source (`patType`) is an LFO running at `patSpeed`. Then `Latch.kr` samples that source every time the trigger fires at `trigSpeed`. Two different clocks.

- With **LFSaw / LFTri / SinOsc**, the source is a *continuous ramp*. Even if the trigger samples it 10 times a second while the saw sweeps once a second, each sample lands at a different point on the ramp — so you hear a moving sequence. patSpeed audibly stretches and squashes that contour.

- With **LFNoise0/1/2**, the source is a *random staircase*. `patSpeed` here means "how often a new random number appears." If `patSpeed` is **lower** than `trigSpeed` — which it is at your defaults (~1–2 Hz vs ~10 Hz) — then the Latch keeps grabbing **the same random value several times in a row** before the noise bothers to change. You hear the same pitch repeated, then a jump, then repeats. That "stuck" feeling is what reads as broken.

Quick way to confirm it's this and not something else: select a noise pattern, then push **patSpeed up high** (above where trigSpeed sits). It should suddenly come alive and sound properly random, because now the noise is generating fresh values faster than the trigger samples them.

There's also a **genuine small inconsistency** feeding into it. Your live slider maps `linlin(0, 1, 0.1, 20)` but the START case (line 1518) still maps `linlin(0, 1, 0, 40)`. So the moment you press start versus the moment you first touch the slider, patSpeed jumps to a different value — and the start mapping can even hit 0. Make them match:

```supercollider
\patSpeed, latchSpeedA.value.linlin(0, 1, 0.1, 20),
```

So: the amp `.max` is a real fix. The noise behaviour is the trigSpeed-vs-patSpeed ratio doing exactly what sample-and-hold does — fixable by taste, not by a bug fix.

Want me to show you a one-line change that makes the noise generators run at `trigSpeed` instead of `patSpeed`, so every trigger always catches a fresh random pitch? That's usually what people actually want from a "random" sequencer step.

[USER]
yes

[CLAUDE]
Small honesty correction — it's three lines, not one, because you've got three noise generators. But it's the same swap on each, so it's one idea.

In the `patType` Select array (lines 1895–1897), the three noise generators currently take `patSpeed` as their first argument — that's their *rate of generating new random values*. Change that first argument from `patSpeed` to `trigSpeed`:

```supercollider
LFNoise0.kr(trigSpeed, latchFreqA, latchFreqB),
LFNoise1.kr(trigSpeed, latchFreqA, latchFreqB),
LFNoise2.kr(trigSpeed, latchFreqA, latchFreqB)
```

Leave the four LFO lines (Sine/Tri/Saw/Pulse) on `patSpeed` — those still want the independent sweep speed.

**Why this works:** now the noise rolls a new random number at the same rate the trigger fires. So when `Latch.kr` samples on each trigger, the noise has (almost always) just changed — every step gets a fresh pitch instead of repeating the last one. The noise patterns become a proper random-per-step sequencer, and `patSpeed` simply has no effect on them, which is honest — for true randomness there's no "pattern speed" to set, only how often you step.

**One caveat to know:** the noise's internal clock and the trigger's `Impulse` clock run independently — both at `trigSpeed`, but not phase-locked. Occasionally the Latch samples a hair before the noise updates and you get one repeat. If that ever bugs you, multiply the noise rate a little:

```supercollider
LFNoise0.kr(trigSpeed * 2, latchFreqA, latchFreqB),
```

That guarantees a new value is always waiting by the time the trigger samples. Start with the plain `trigSpeed` version, listen, and only add the `* 2` if you hear repeats.

Give it a run and tell me how the noise patterns feel now.

Now I've got the full picture. That's four distinct features, and the order matters because one of them (detune) restructures the signal in a way that affects the others. Here's the sequence I'd suggest:

1. **Coarse tune** — simplest. One arg, applied to the latched frequency. Affects every oscillator, always visible.
2. **PMOsc + SinOscFB** — add both to the osc `SelectX` array, each with their own params and show/hide controls (like Pulse/Blip/Formant).

**Step 1 — coarse tune in the SynthDef.**

First, add the arg. Find line 1882 (the arg list) and add `coarse=0` to it:
```supercollider
arg out=0, latchFreqA=200, latchFreqB=500, patSpeed=1, trigSpeed=10, cut=1000,q=0.5, gate=1, verbSend=0,delaySend=0, latchAmp=1,pan=0.0, patType=0, trigType=0, oscType=0, filterType=0, pulseWidth=0.5, nHarm=4, formFreq=1760, bwFreq=880, coarse=0;
```

Then find line 1903:
```supercollider
sig = Latch.kr(sig, trig*env1);
```
Add a line directly after it:
```supercollider
sig = sig * coarse.midiratio;
```

`coarse` is a number of semitones, and `.midiratio` converts semitones into a frequency multiplier — so `12.midiratio` = 2.0 (an octave up), `(-12).midiratio` = 0.5 (an octave down), `7.midiratio` ≈ 1.5 (a fifth). Multiplying the latched frequency by that transposes the whole sequence musically rather than by a flat number of Hz.

Tell me when that's in.

These two are FM-style/feedback oscillators that bring a much grittier, more metallic palette than the basic waveforms. Let's do the SynthDef first.

**Step 2a — add args and the two oscillators.**

First, add three args. Find the arg list (line 1882) and add `pmRatio=1, pmIndex=1, fbAmount=0.5` to the end:
```supercollider
arg out=0, latchFreqA=200, latchFreqB=500, patSpeed=1, trigSpeed=10, cut=1000,q=0.5, gate=1, verbSend=0,delaySend=0, latchAmp=1,pan=0.0, patType=0, trigType=0, oscType=0, filterType=0, pulseWidth=0.5, nHarm=4, formFreq=1760, bwFreq=880, coarse=0, pmRatio=1, pmIndex=1, fbAmount=0.5;
```

Then in the osc `SelectX` array, find the `Formant` line (line 1911). Add a comma after it and two new lines:
```supercollider
					Formant.ar(sig,formFreq, bwFreq, 0.2)*env2,
					PMOsc.ar(sig, sig*pmRatio, pmIndex, 0, 0.2)*env2,
					SinOscFB.ar(sig, fbAmount, 0.2)*env2
```

A bit about each:
- **PMOsc** is phase-modulation (the heart of FM synthesis). `sig` is the carrier, `sig*pmRatio` is the modulator — tying the modulator to a *ratio* of the carrier keeps it harmonic as the pitch sequences. `pmIndex` is the modulation depth — low = nearly pure tone, high = bright and clangorous.
- **SinOscFB** is a sine that feeds its own output back into its phase. `fbAmount` from 0 (pure sine) up toward ~pi grows from sine → sawtooth-ish → noisy. Great for evolving timbres.

Tell me when that's in.

**Bug 1 — the actual cause of your error.** Line 1349: the feedback label is assigned to the wrong variable name:
```supercollider
latchPmIndexLabelA  = StaticText(latchSynthViewA, Rect(270, 70, 70, 23))
```

That says `latchPmIndexLabelA` but it should be `latchFbLabelA`. Because of this, `latchFbLabelA` never gets assigned — it stays `nil` — so when the menu action calls `latchFbLabelA.visible_(...)`, you get "Message 'visible_' not understood" (nil doesn't understand it). Change line 1349 to:

**Bug 2 — missing underscore.** Line 1431:
```supercollider
latchFbLabelA.visible(false);
```

That's the getter, not the setter. Change to:
```supercollider
latchFbLabelA.visible_(false);
```

**While you're there — one more, line 1347:** `.value(0.08)` is also missing its underscore. It should be `.value_(0.08)` or the feedback knob won't pick up its starting position.

One small heads-up, not a bug: line 1345 maps feedback `linlin(0, 1, 0, 12)`. SinOscFB goes chaotic/noisy above about pi (~3.14), so the top three-quarters of that knob will all be roughly "noise". Totally fine if that's the palette you want — just know the musical range lives in the bottom quarter. If you'd rather have finer control, `linlin(0,1,0,4)` keeps it near the useful zone.

### 4. Structural Overview

**Architecture: 6-slot single-synth workstation (Slot A only; B–F slots not implemented)**

**Mode Switching (typeMenuA, 0–5):**
- Each mode hides all OTHER views and shows only its own
- GUI visibility logic is **self-consistent** across all 6 cases (verified lines 468–553)
- Case 0 (Grains): Shows grain controls + position slider
- Case 1 (Loop): Shows PlayBuf controls; hides position slider
- Case 2 (Warp): Shows Warp1 controls
- Case 3 (Image): Shows imageScanSpeedA slider + row/synth-mode selectors; launches image scan routine
- Case 4 (Record): Shows recButtonA + input channel menu; **no synth handler** ← BUG
- Case 5 (Seq-S&amp;H): Shows latchViewA (pattern controls) + latchSynthViewA (osc/filter controls)

**Image Mode (Case 3) Subsystem:**
- 5 selectable synth defs via imageSynthModeA menu: Sine / SineRGB / Pluck / Saw / Square
- imageSynthTrigA array controls whether each spawns fresh synth-per-pixel (true) or modulates one sustained synth (false)
- Scan routine loops through image pixels, converting brightness → freq, reads at imageScanRowA height
- **Issue:** imagePan always defaults to 0; no GUI control

**Latch/Seq Mode (Case 5) Subsystem:**
- Pattern LFO (7 types: Sine/Tri/Saw/Pulse/Noise0/Noise1/Noise2) modulates frequency range (latchFreqA to latchFreqB)
- Latch triggers on Impulse or Dust; EnvGen shapes the attack
- 8 oscillator types selectable (Sine/Tri/Saw/Pulse/Blip/Formant/PM/SineFB) with mode-dependent controls
  - Pulse: pulseWidth knob (visible only when menu.value == 3)
  - Blip: nHarm knob (visible only when menu.value == 4)
  - Formant: formFreq + bwFreq (visible when menu.value == 5)
  - PM: pmRatio + pmIndex (visible when menu.value == 6)
  - SineFB: fbAmount (visible when menu.value == 7)
- 7 filter types (RLPF/RHPF/DFM1/MoogVCF/MoogLadder/GlitchRHPF/Resonz) with cut and rez knobs
- **Visibility logic verified lines 1277–1290:** Each osc-type case correctly shows/hides its controls ✓

**Effects Chain:**
- Two send buses: busReverb, busDelay
- FreeVerb and CombN run at tail order (lower priority than voice synths)
- Master record button records stereo output to disk

[CLAUDE]
Here's the plan for detune, following the same idea we used for coarse tune but going one step further.

**The idea:** instead of playing one oscillator at the exact latched pitch, we'll play *two* copies of it — one very slightly flat, one very slightly sharp — and mix them together. When two nearly-identical pitches sound at once, they drift in and out of phase with each other, creating that shimmering, thickened "chorus" quality. The trick in SuperCollider is that if you feed an oscillator a *2-element array* as its frequency instead of a single number, SC automatically builds two separate copies of that oscillator running in parallel — one per array element. We don't need to touch the oscillator code at all; we just need to turn the frequency into a pair before it gets there, then fold the two results back into one signal afterward.

**Step 1 — add the arg and split the frequency into a pair.**

Find line 1967 (the arg list) and add `detune=0` to the end:
```supercollider
arg out=0, latchFreqA=200, latchFreqB=500, patSpeed=1, trigSpeed=10, cut=1000,q=0.5, gate=1, verbSend=0,delaySend=0, latchAmp=1,pan=0.0, patType=0, trigType=0, oscType=0, filterType=0, pulseWidth=0.5, nHarm=4, formFreq=1760, bwFreq=880, coarse=0, pmRatio=1, pmIndex=1, fbAmount=0.5, detune=0;
```

Then find line 1989:
```supercollider
sig = sig * coarse.midiratio;
```
Add directly after it:
```supercollider
sig = sig * [1 - detune, 1 + detune];
```

`detune` will be a small fraction like `0.005` (half a percent). `[1 - detune, 1 + detune]` gives you something like `[0.995, 1.005]` — one copy 0.5% flat, one copy 0.5% sharp. Multiplying the frequency by that array is what causes SC to spawn two parallel copies of whatever oscillator comes next.

Tell me when that's in.

[USER]
ok

[CLAUDE]
**Step 2 — fold the two detuned voices back into one signal.**

Right after the oscillator `SelectX` block, we need to sum the two channels back down to one before the signal continues to the filter. Find line 2001 (the closing of the osc `SelectX` block):
```supercollider
]);
```
That's the one right after the `SinOscFB` line. Directly after it, before the filter section starts, add:
```supercollider
sig = sig.sum * 0.5;
```

`.sum` adds the two channels together into one. The `* 0.5` compensates for the fact that adding two signals together roughly doubles the loudness — this brings it back down to a comparable level to before, so the detune doesn't suddenly make everything much louder.

Tell me when that's in.

[CLAUDE]
Now for distortion. Quick design note before we start: distortion will sit in the signal chain *after* the oscillator stage, applying to whatever comes out of it — which means if we only hide the control for non-Sine/Tri oscillators without resetting it, a leftover "Fold" setting could keep silently mangling your Saw or Blip sound with no visible control showing why. To avoid that gotcha, I'll have the oscillator menu automatically snap distortion back to "Clean" whenever you switch away from Sine or Tri. That keeps things honest — what you see in the GUI always matches what you hear.

Here's the plan, same shape as the image synths' distortion (Clean/Clip/Fold/Wrap):

**Step 1 — add the arg and the distortion Select.**

Find your arg list (currently ending in `detune=0`) and add `distortType=0` to it:
```supercollider
arg out=0, latchFreqA=200, latchFreqB=500, patSpeed=1, trigSpeed=10, cut=1000,q=0.5, gate=1, verbSend=0,delaySend=0, latchAmp=1,pan=0.0, patType=0, trigType=0, oscType=0, filterType=0, pulseWidth=0.5, nHarm=4, formFreq=1760, bwFreq=880, coarse=0, pmRatio=1, pmIndex=1, fbAmount=0.5, detune=0, distortType=0;
```

Then find the line you added a few steps ago:
```supercollider
sig = sig.sum * 0.5;
```
Add directly after it:
```supercollider
sig = Select.ar(distortType, [
    sig,
    Clip.ar(sig, 0, 1),
    Fold.ar(sig, 0, 1),
    Wrap.ar(sig, 0, 1)
]);
```

This goes right after the chorus-sum and right before the filter section — so distortion happens to the combined chorused tone, then gets filtered afterward, same order as the image synths.

Tell me when that's in.

ok. what I want to do is go back to the filter and change the cutoff and rez amounts depending on the type of filter selected eg, RHPF value is 1 and q is 1 but then the switch to say moog is cut.value = 1, and q = 0, etc

[CLAUDE]
Good instinct — some of these filters interpret "resonance" completely differently, and a couple of them (Moog-style filters especially) can self-oscillate and get uncomfortably loud if you push resonance too far. Let me look at the current filter block again to make sure I've got the exact argument order right before proposing ranges.

[CLAUDE]
Good — you've actually already flagged both of the exact problems that make this necessary: your own comment at line 1061 notes "RLPF q is backwards," and your filter menu label already screams "GlitchRHPF-CAREFUL-LOUD!!" at line 1397. That's precisely the situation where one knob position means something completely different (and sometimes dangerously loud) depending on which filter is active.

**The plan:** rather than changing what gets sent from the GUI, we'll do the remapping *inside the SynthDef* itself. The knob keeps sending a plain 0–1 value for `q` like it does now — but inside the SynthDef, we add one `Select.kr(filterType, [...])` block that turns that raw 0–1 into the correct number for whichever filter is currently chosen. This is safe and free to do because of how `Select` already works here: every filter branch gets computed regardless, but only the active one's *output* reaches your ears — so it's fine that the "wrong" filters also receive a value, since nobody's listening to them.

Here's my proposed mapping per filter, correcting each one so that **turning the knob up always means "more resonant," consistently**:

- **RLPF** (fixes your "backwards" note): rq goes from 1 (no resonance) at knob-0 down to 0.1 (resonant) at knob-1
- **RHPF**: same convention as RLPF
- **DFM1**: its `res` arg is already a natural 0–1, resonant-when-high — passes through unchanged
- **MoogVCF**: I'm less certain of this one's exact self-oscillation point from memory — I'd suggest capping it around 2.5 out of a possible 0–4 range as a conservative start
- **MoogLadder**: resonance 0–1, self-oscillates near 1 — I'd cap around 0.9 so there's still headroom before it screams
- **GlitchRHPF**: same rq idea as RHPF, but since you've already marked this one as dangerous, I'd give it an even safer floor — maybe never going below rq 0.3
- **Resonz**: its third argument is bandwidth ratio, where *smaller* is narrower/more resonant — so knob-up should map to a small number, roughly 2 down to 0.05

I'm confident about RLPF/RHPF/DFM1/Resonz; less certain about the exact numbers for the two Moog filters and Glitch, since those come from external UGen collections rather than the SC core — so those three are a "start here and listen carefully" starting point, not gospel.

One more design question before we start: **when you switch filter type while something's already playing, should the q knob automatically snap back to a safe low position** (say 0), rather than carrying over whatever position it was left at? That would stop a resonance setting that was safe on RLPF from suddenly becoming very hot the instant you flip to Moog or Glitch. I'd recommend yes, given you've already had to warn yourself about one of these — but it's your call.

| index | actual filter | your `qMapped` entry |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | RLPF | RLPF ✓ |
| 1 | RHPF | RHPF ✓ |
| 2 | DFM1 | DFM1 ✓ |

| 5 | GlitchRHPF | *(gets Resonz's mapping instead)* |
| 6 | Resonz | *(no slot — falls back to the last entry, which by luck happens to still be right)* |

The `MoogLadder` line got skipped entirely, which shunts everything below it up by one slot. ```supercollider

```supercollider
    q.linlin(0, 1, 1, 0.1),    // RLPF
    q.linlin(0, 1, 1, 0.1),    // RHPF
    q.linlin(0, 1, 0, 1),      // DFM1
    q.linlin(0, 1, 0, 2.5),    // MoogVCF
    q.linlin(0, 1, 0, 0.9),    // MoogLadder
    q.linlin(0, 1, 1, 0.3),    // GlitchRHPF
    q.linlin(0, 1, 2, 0.05),   // Resonz
]);
```

Now for the second part — making the GUI *show* the reset, not just apply it silently.

**The idea:** when you switch filter type, we'll snap the resonance knob back to its `0` position — the safe end for every one of the 7 mappings we just built, since I deliberately designed all seven so that knob-0 always means "least resonant." The trick is using `.valueAction_()` instead of `.value_()`: `.value_()` alone would move the knob visually but wouldn't actually notify the running synth of the change, while `.valueAction_()` does both — it moves the knob *and* fires the knob's own `.action_` code, exactly as if you'd dragged it there yourself.

```supercollider
latchQA.refresh;
```

`.refresh` just forces the widget to repaint itself based on its current internal value — it's a very common one-line fix for exactly this "value changed but the picture didn't" situation.

[CLAUDE]
That's a different, trickier symptom — the underlying value and sound are correctly updating, but the knob's on-screen picture isn't following. This points to a threading quirk in SC's GUI toolkit: even though a `PopUpMenu` action *usually* runs safely on the GUI thread, nested calls that make one widget's action reach into and change *another* widget mid-callback can sometimes get dropped by Qt's paint cycle. The fix is to schedule that update for the very next GUI tick instead of doing it inline — the same `.defer` trick we used earlier for the input meter, just in a new context.

Find your filter menu action:
```supercollider
.action_({|menu|
    synthAStart.set(\filterType, menu.value);
    latchQA.valueAction_(0);
    latchQA.refresh;
});
```

Change it to:
```supercollider
.action_({|menu|
    synthAStart.set(\filterType, menu.value);
    {
        latchQA.valueAction_(0);
    }.defer;
});
```

`.defer` here means "don't run this right now — wait for SC's next free GUI moment, then run it." That gives Qt a clean opportunity to actually repaint the knob rather than trying to do it mid-event.

Find your `latchFilterMenuA` action and change the reset line from a flat `0` to a check for which filter was picked:
```supercollider
.action_({|menu|

        latchQA.valueAction_(if(menu.value <= 1, 1, 0));
    }.defer;
});
```

`menu.value <= 1` catches indices 0 and 1 — RLPF and RHPF — and resets those to `1`. Everything else (DFM1 through Resonz) still resets to `0`, matching their own safe end.

One honest side effect worth knowing: this means RLPF and RHPF's knob will now feel "backwards" relative to the other five — turning it up will make those two *less* resonant while turning up makes the rest *more* resonant. That's the direct consequence of what you just described, so I want to flag it plainly rather than let it surprise you later.

**Step 2 — add the gain knob.**

There's a gap in `recViewA` between the jump menu (ending around x=340) and the level meter (starting at x=520) — plenty of room. Add this after the `meterA` line:

```supercollider
inputGainA = Knob(recViewA, Rect(380, 5, 50, 50))
.action_({|kb|
    var gain = kb.value.linlin(0, 1, -20, 20).dbamp;
    if(recSynthA.notNil, { recSynthA.set(\inGain, gain) });
    meterSynthA.set(\inGain, gain);
})
.value_(0.5);
StaticText(recViewA, Rect(380, 58, 50, 20))
.string_("gain")
.background_(Color.grey(0.7, 0.5))
.align_(\center);
```

A couple of things worth explaining here. The mapping goes through decibels rather than a plain linear range — `kb.value.linlin(0, 1, -20, 20)` turns the knob position into a dB value from -20 to +20, and `.dbamp` converts that dB value into the actual multiplier `SoundIn.ar` needs. This matches how a real gain trim knob feels: the exact centre of the knob (0.5, which is also its starting default) lands on 0dB — meaning unity, no change at all — turning right boosts, turning left cuts.

The `if(recSynthA.notNil, ...)` guard matters because `recSynthA` only exists while you're actively mid-recording — if you're just sitting there adjusting gain before pressing record, there's no recording synth yet to update, but `meterSynthA` always exists once the workstation has booted, so it's safe to set unconditionally.

Tell me when that's in.

[USER]
ok. I would also like to create a new sequencer using the stepper. a bit like the Latch, but in its new window. 
Also, some kind of master modulation sync???

[CLAUDE]
Two good but fairly open ideas — let me think through each before we touch any code.

**The Stepper sequencer.** `Stepper.kr` is genuinely different from `Latch.kr` in a way that matters: Latch just grabs whatever value a free-running LFO happens to be at when triggered — so the pitch sequence is somewhat unpredictable, shaped by the LFO's shape and speed. `Stepper` instead counts cleanly through a fixed range (0, 1, 2, 3... wrapping back to 0), which is exactly what you need to *individually* set the pitch of step 1, step 2, step 3, etc. — a proper hands-on step sequencer, like a small hardware sequencer where each step has its own dial. The natural SC widget for that is `MultiSliderView` — a single widget showing a row of adjustable bars side by side, one per step, which you'd read with `Select.kr(Stepper.kr(...), [...])` or similar. That's a new GUI class for you, worth learning.

Given how well it worked to build the Latch sequencer in stages — bare working version first, then oscillators, then filters, then all the safety/detune/distortion polish — I'd suggest the same approach here: get a basic Stepper engine with plain pitch steps and one simple tone working first, in its own window, before adding switchable oscillators/filters on top.

Two things I need from you before we start:
1. **How many steps** — 8 is the classic small step-sequencer size, 16 is the classic "full bar" size. Either is easy to build, just want to know which to wire up first.
2. **Does it live inside the same effects/output world as everything else** — i.e. does it send to the shared reverb/delay buses and use the same output-channel selector pattern as Grains/Loop/Warp/Image/Seq — or is this new window meant to be a fully separate, self-contained thing with no connection to the rest of the workstation?

**Master modulation sync.** Since you put "???" after it yourself — my read is this means something like: right now, every sequencer (Latch, and soon Stepper) runs its own free-running clock internally, so if you had two sequencers going at once, their steps would drift in and out of phase with each other over time, never quite locking together. A "master sync" would mean one shared clock that multiple synths can optionally lock their step-rate to, so everything stays rhythmically related instead of drifting.

That's a real feature, but it only becomes meaningful once there are *two* independent sequencers to sync — right now there's just Latch. I'd suggest tabling this until the Stepper sequencer exists, then coming back to it, rather than designing a sync system for hypothetical future instruments. Does that sequencing make sense, or did you have something more specific in mind for "sync"?

[USER]
do you know about the buchla 5 step mode?

[CLAUDE]
I know the broad idea, though I'd flag my confidence on exact specifics as moderate rather than certain — the Buchla 200-series sequencer modules (like the 246) are famous for a different philosophy than the typical 8/16-step linear sequencer: instead of one long row of steps, they're often organized as **two rows of 5 stages**, which can run as two independent 5-stage sequences at once, or be linked into a single longer sequence, and each stage can carry more than just a pitch — often a separate pulse/gate output too, plus options to skip or repeat stages. The appeal is partly aesthetic (it comes from Buchla's "West Coast" approach of treating a sequencer as a generator of interesting *events* rather than a rigid grid) and partly musical — 5-based groupings create polyrhythms and asymmetric phrases that a power-of-two sequencer (8, 16) naturally can't, since 5 doesn't divide evenly against the usual 4/8/16 pulse.

If that's the direction you're drawn to, it changes the design meaningfully from my "8 or 16 steps" question — instead we'd likely want:
- Two `Stepper.kr`s, each wrapping 0–4 (5 stages), rather than one big linear counter
- A way to run them independently, or link them into a combined 10-stage sequence
- Possibly per-stage skip/enable, if you want that classic "sequence with holes in it" feel

Is that the kind of thing you're picturing — two independent or linkable 5-stage rows — or were you thinking of something more specific from the Buchla you've used?

[USER]
lets make a plan for the 6 channel port

[CLAUDE]
There's one fork that needs deciding before anything else, because it determines the shape of every stage after it.

## The fork: how the 6 channels get built

**Path 1 — copy-paste, six named copies.** This is what the old `V0_1_5.scd` file already started (`\grainA` through `\grainF`), and it's also how the current single-channel file's leftover `var` declarations are structured (`ampSliderA/B/C/D/E/F` etc. were scaffolded from early on, unused since). Every widget, every action, every variable gets typed out six times with a different letter suffix. Concretely: the current single-channel file is 2454 lines; six literal copies of everything currently in it would put the file somewhere around 12,000–15,000 lines. Each new feature added later (say, a 9th oscillator on the Latch sequencer) needs the same edit made in six places by hand.

**Path 2 — one reusable builder, called six times.** Instead of `ampSliderA`, `ampSliderB`, etc. as separate variables, you'd have one array, `ampSliders`, with `ampSliders[0]` through `ampSliders[5]`. One function builds "a channel's worth of GUI and logic," parameterized by a channel index, and gets called in a loop six times. Total code size stays much closer to the current single-channel file (something like 3000–4000 lines, not 12,000+), and a fix or new feature only needs to happen once. The cost is a real rewrite of what already works for channel A — it doesn't get reused as-is, it gets restructured to fit the array/function pattern — and it introduces array-based widget access as a new concept, on top of everything else you've learned so far.

Both are legitimate engineering choices with real, different consequences — that's your call to make, not mine.

## What's already shared vs. what needs replicating per channel

Already channel-agnostic, needs little or no change either way:
- The audio setup/boot window (one device selection for the whole engine)
- `busReverb` / `busDelay` (shared effect buses — each channel just needs its own send-amount knob into them, which is already how channel A works)

Needs full replication (whichever path is chosen), from smallest to largest:
- Master panel strip: fader, output selector, level meter (channel A already has a working version of each)
- Grains / Loop / Warp modes (single-synth, relatively simple)
- Image mode (scan routine, row selector, 5 switchable synth types)
- Record mode (input channel picker, gain knob, meter, auto-jump)
- Latch/Seq mode — by far the largest single piece: 7 pattern sources, 2 trigger types, 8 oscillators, 7 filters with individually safety-mapped resonance, detune, distortion

## A staged build order, with real stopping points

Given how the single-channel version got built — one mode at a time, each one working before moving to the next — the same shape makes sense here, and it means the port doesn't have to be all-or-nothing before it's useful:

1. Lock in the architecture (Path 1 or 2) and prove it out with just Grains across all 6 channels
2. Add Loop and Warp to all 6
3. Add Image mode to all 6
4. Add Record mode to all 6
5. Add Latch/Seq to all 6 (the big one)
6. Wire up the 6-strip master panel (faders, output routing, meters)

Each numbered stage leaves you with a working, playable instrument — even stopping after stage 2 gives you 6 channels of Grains/Loop/Warp, which is already more than the current single channel offers in that dimension.

Which path do you want for the fork — copy-paste or the reusable builder?

[CLAUDE]
Before the very first line of slot B, there's a layout question worth settling, because it changes how much rework every single widget position needs.

Right now, `win` (the window holding the soundfile view and the whole Grains/Loop/Warp/Image/Record/Seq switcher) and `masterWin` (the fader/output/meter strip) are both already full of hardcoded `Rect(x, y, w, h)` positions sized for one slot. There are two ways to fit slot B in:

**Give slot B its own separate window** — duplicate the entire `win = Window(...)` block as a new `winB`, keeping every single `Rect()` position exactly as-is (since it's a fresh, empty window, slot A's coordinates work unchanged for slot B too). `masterWin` stays as one shared mixer panel, and each slot just adds its own fader/selector/meter strip to it, positioned next to slot A's. This means copy-pasting slot A's window code needs almost no coordinate math — just renaming.

**Cram slot B into the same `win`**, stacked below or beside slot A's layout — meaning recalculating a Y-offset (or X-offset) for every single one of the hundreds of `Rect()` calls in slot A's code so they don't overlap. Much more arithmetic, much more room for a stray un-shifted widget to land on top of an existing one.

Given you're doing this the long way by hand, the separate-window approach is a lot less error-prone purely mechanically — but it's your call, since it also changes how the finished instrument looks and feels to perform with (six separate windows to arrange on screen vs. one big one). Which do you want?

[CLAUDE]
Confirmed — I checked V0_1_5 directly: it uses one shared `win`, with each slot's soundfile view and subwin stacked at increasing Y positions (slot A starts at y=10, slot B at y=180 — a 170px vertical band per slot).

One thing worth flagging before we reuse that number: V0_1_5's slot A was just a bare Grains player when that 170px spacing was set. The current slot A has grown enormously since — Image mode, Record mode, and especially the Latch/Seq system with its oscillator and filter panels — all of which need far more vertical room than a bare Grains player did. Reusing 170px per slot would almost certainly cause slot B's widgets to overlap slot A's.

Do you want to actually measure the current slot A's full vertical extent to get an exact number, or just pick a generously large round figure (say 500 or 600px per slot) and adjust later if something clips?

That wall of errors is a real, specific thing — and it's actually a very informative one for the plan we just made. Let me verify my hypothesis by counting how many variables are actually declared in the file before saying anything for certain.

[CLAUDE]
Found it — saved as `marrsWorkstationV02_5_1.scd` (matching the established file-naming convention). Let me check its size and variable count.

[CLAUDE]
That confirms it precisely, and it's important. This isn't a typo anywhere — it's a hard ceiling in the SuperCollider language itself.

**What's actually happening:** every single variable in that error list — `busReverb`, `busDelay`, `log`, `reverb`, `delay`, `masterMeter`, `meterA`, `verbView`, `delayView` — has been working perfectly for this entire project. Nothing about them changed. What changed is *how many other variables now exist before them* in the same block. I counted: by the time the `var` declarations reach line 309 (`log`) and line 311 (`busReverb`/`busDelay`/etc.), roughly 300+ separate variable names have already been declared earlier in that same giant enclosing function. SuperCollider's compiler has a hard, fixed-size limit on how many local variables a single compiled function can hold — and once that ceiling is crossed, it doesn't error loudly at the point of the 301st variable. Instead, every `var` declared *after* the ceiling silently fails to register at all, which is why everything declared from that point onward — even old, solid, previously-working code — comes back as "not defined."

This lines up exactly with what happened: duplicating slot A's already-large variable set into a matching slot B roughly doubled the total count, and that doubling is what pushed the whole block over the edge.

The important part for the plan we made: **this has nothing to do with care or typos.** No amount of careful copy-pasting gets around a hard ceiling in the language itself. And it means Path 1 as originally conceived — six full sets of separately-named variables, all living in one block — isn't just tedious, it's structurally incapable of reaching all 6 slots. Slot B alone already tipped it over.

This is, in a very real sense, the exact lesson Path 2 exists to solve — an array like `ampSliders[0..5]` costs *one* variable slot no matter how many channels it holds, sidestepping the ceiling entirely, whereas `ampSliderA` through `ampSliderF` costs six.

A few real ways forward from here, factually, no steer from me on which:
1. **Switch to the array/collection-based approach (Path 2) now**, before pushing further into slot C onward, since continuing with separately-named variables will keep hitting the same wall, worse each time.
2. **Split the code into multiple separate evaluated blocks** (each its own compiled function, its own variable budget) — but this runs headlong into your no-globals rule, since sharing things like `busReverb`, `log`, or the server reference across separately-evaluated blocks normally needs either a global or some other bridging mechanism.
3. **Bundle related variables into a single Event/dictionary per channel** (e.g. one `chanB` variable holding `chanB.ampSlider`, `chanB.latchTrigSpeed`, etc. as keys) — a middle ground that only costs one `var` slot per channel while still reading somewhat name-like rather than fully numeric-array-like.

How do you want to proceed?

That's fair — it's a real fork and there's no obviously "correct" answer. Let me ask one thing that might help it click, rather than pile on more options.

Your original plan was to do Path 1 the long way round specifically so you'd *feel* why Path 2 might be worth it, rather than just being told. You've now hit a wall that answers that question about as clearly as it could be answered — separately-named variables genuinely cannot scale to 6 slots in one block, full stop. That's not a maybe, it's a hard ceiling you've now bumped into directly.

Good — task 1 is where we start. First, a quick practical note: the `B`-suffixed variable declarations already sitting in the file (`ampSliderB`, `latchTrigSpeedB`, etc.) become dead weight now — they were part of what pushed you over the ceiling in the first place, and they won't be used once things move to arrays. We don't need to rip them all out in one terrifying pass — we can delete each group as we replace it with its array equivalent, feature by feature, so it stays manageable.

Now, the core new idea for Path 2, explained with the actual variables you already know:

Right now you have `bufferA, bufferB, bufferC, bufferD, bufferE, bufferF` — six separate variable names, each one a separate slot SuperCollider has to track. The array approach replaces all six with **one** variable, `buffers`, which holds 6 *slots inside itself* rather than 6 separate named variables. `buffers[0]` is what used to be `bufferA`. `buffers[1]` is what used to be `bufferB`. And so on through `buffers[5]` for slot F. To SuperCollider's compiler, this is just **one** variable — no matter how many channels it holds inside. That's the whole trick that sidesteps the ceiling.

**Step 2 — initialize the arrays and build the loop.**

Find where `win = Window(...)` finishes being set up (around line 351, `win.background=Color.green(0.2);`). Add this directly after it:

```supercollider
buffers = Array.newClear(6);
soundFileViews = Array.newClear(6);
subwins = Array.newClear(6);
soundFiles = Array.newClear(6);
openfiles = Array.newClear(6);
startButtons = Array.newClear(6);
synthsStart = Array.newClear(6);
posSliderViews = Array.newClear(6);

6.do({|i|
    var rowY = 10 + (i * 250);

    soundFileViews[i] = SoundFileView(win, Rect(10, rowY, sfViewWidth, sfViewHeight))
    .gridOn_(true)
    .timeCursorOn_(true)
    .timeCursorColor_(Color.white)
    .timeCursorPosition_(100);
    soundFileViews[i].visible_(true);

    subwins[i] = CompositeView.new(win, Rect(sfViewWidth+20, rowY, sfViewWidth/1.3, sfViewHeight+45))
    .background_(Color.yellow(0.3));
});
```

A few things worth explaining: `Array.newClear(6)` makes a 6-slot array pre-filled with `nil`, ready for us to fill in by index. The `6.do({|i| ...})` loop runs that block 6 times, with `i` counting `0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5` — each pass builds one channel's soundfile view and subwin container, at a different vertical position (`rowY`) so they don't overlap on screen. `soundFileViews[i]` and `subwins[i]` are exactly equivalent to what `soundFileViewA`/`subwinA` used to be, just reached by number instead of by letter.

I moved the `.gridOn_`/`.timeCursorOn_` setup into this block since that's how the original `soundFileViewA` was configured elsewhere in the file — check when you get there that we haven't duplicated anything.

Type that in and tell me what you get — it should draw 6 empty soundfile view boxes stacked down the left side of the window, each with an empty yellow subwin box to its right.

[CLAUDE]
That confirms it — every single error there is one of the old `A`-suffixed names (`soundFileViewA`, `subwinA`, `bufferA`, `synthAStart`, `startButtonA`, and so on). Here's honestly what happened: Step 1 removed the *declarations* for all those names, but the rest of the file — Grains, Loop, Warp, Image, Record, Latch/Seq, all of it — still *uses* those old names in hundreds of places. We only updated where they were declared and where the soundfile view itself gets created; we didn't yet update everywhere they're referenced. That's on me for not flagging clearly enough that removing a declaration breaks every place that name is still used elsewhere.

Given the scale of this — nearly every feature in the file touches `synthAStart` or `startButtonA` in some way — the fix has to happen the same way: feature by feature, mechanically swapping the old name for its array equivalent (`bufferA` → `buffers[i]`, `synthAStart` → `synthsStart[i]`, etc.), following the same task breakdown we already have. The file **will not compile** until each pass is done — that's not a new problem appearing each time, it's just the nature of this particular refactor: you can't test it piece by piece the way we've tested everything else this whole project, because SuperCollider needs every variable reference in the block resolved before any of it runs.

Let's start with the foundational renames — the ones used almost everywhere, not just in one mode:

**`startButtonA` → `startButtons[i]` and `synthAStart` → `synthsStart[i]`** are used by every single mode (Grains, Loop, Warp, Image, Record, Seq all read/set these), so they belong in the foundation pass alongside the soundfile view.

Since these appear in dozens of places throughout the file, the most reliable way to do this is a careful **find-and-replace**, one name at a time, checking each match rather than blindly replacing all — because a couple of these have look-alike neighbors (e.g. `synthAStart` vs. things like `synthAStart.notNil`, which still needs the same swap, just watch you don't accidentally catch a different variable that happens to contain the same substring).

Here's the full list to work through for the foundation pass, each old name → its new array form:
- `soundFileViewA` → `soundFileViews[i]`
- `subwinA` → `subwins[i]`
- `bufferA` → `buffers[i]`
- `soundFileA` → `soundFiles[i]`
- `openfileA` → `openfiles[i]`
- `startButtonA` → `startButtons[i]`
- `synthAStart` → `synthsStart[i]`
- `posSilderViewA` → `posSliderViews[i]` (small bonus: this also quietly fixes an old typo — "Silder" was always a misspelling of "Slider" — worth fixing now since we're touching every reference anyway)

Given how much code this touches, do you want to go through this find-and-replace yourself in your editor (searching for each old name one at a time), or would it help if I first located every remaining occurrence of each name and gave you the exact line numbers to work through?

[USER]
i'll do it for now

[CLAUDE]
Sounds good. Take your time working through it — since it's one big consistent pass (same 8 names, swapped everywhere they appear), it should go fairly quickly once you get into the rhythm of it. Ping me when you've done a pass and want to check the errors again, or if you hit something confusing partway through.

[CLAUDE]
That "Index not an Integer" line is the real clue — something is writing into `subwins` or `posSliderViews` (both currently hold exactly this: 6 already-built CompositeViews) using an index that isn't a plain whole number. Since this is a runtime error (not a compile error), it means everything now compiles fine — this is a logic bug happening after the fact, likely something scheduled (the call stack shows it firing from inside a deferred/scheduled callback, not the main top-to-bottom build). Let me search for every place either array gets written to.

[CLAUDE]
Line 443 is a red flag — there's a *second* assignment to `subwins[i]`, separate from the one in our original loop at line 372. Let me look at the actual bracket structure between them to understand what's really going on.

[CLAUDE]
Found it, and it's a genuinely tricky SuperCollider gotcha worth understanding properly — this isn't really about `subwins` vs `posSliderViews` specifically, it's about how `i` is behaving outside a loop.

**The root cause:** SuperCollider has a special rule for single lowercase letters (`a` through `z`) inside any `{...}` function: if you use one of these letters without declaring it, SuperCollider doesn't throw a "not defined" error the way it would for a longer name like `bufferCount`. Instead, it silently treats that letter as one of the function's own **implicit arguments** — the same mechanism that lets you write `{|a, b| a + b}` but shortened further. Since the giant `{...}` block passed to `s.doWhenBooted` is never called with any arguments, every one of its implicit letter-arguments (`a`, `b`, `c`... including `i`) defaults to `nil`.

So here's what's actually happening: back at line 372, inside the loop `6.do({|i| ...})`, that `i` is a proper loop variable counting 0 through 5 — completely correct there. But at line 443, `subwins[i] = ...` sits **outside** that loop, in the outer function's scope. The `i` there isn't connected to the loop at all — it's the outer function's own nil-valued implicit argument. So `subwins[i]` becomes `subwins[nil]`, and writing to an array slot with `nil` as the index is exactly what "`_BasicPut` failed: Index not an Integer" means.

**What this means for the fix:** everything currently using `[i]` outside of lines 362–374 and 403–410 — that's `subwins[i]` (443, 448, 452, 456, 461, 465, 469, 476, 1554, 1567, 1587), `openfiles[i]` (638), `posSliderViews[i]` (693) — is relying on an `i` that doesn't actually mean what it looks like it means. Since all of this code is meant to build one channel's *entire* setup (soundfile view, subwin, type switcher, every mode, the per-channel master strip), the real fix is to **merge all of it into one single `6.do({|i| ...})` loop** that wraps the whole per-channel construction, rather than having several small disconnected loops and a lot of code sitting outside any loop at all. The two small loops we already have (362–374, 403–410) should fold into this one larger loop rather than staying separate.

This is a bigger restructuring job than a one-line fix, so rather than me describing every brace to move, does it make sense for you to find where "channel A's" construction genuinely begins (right after the master panel/quit button, around line 443) and ends, and wrap that whole stretch in one `6.do({|i| ...})`, removing the two smaller loops' own boundaries along the way? Or would it help if I first mapped out exactly where that stretch starts and ends before you touch anything?

[CLAUDE]
Take a breath — here's exactly where things stand, nothing more.

**Why we're doing any of this.** You have one working channel — `marrsWorkstationV02_4_2.scd` — with everything you've built over many sessions: Grains, Loop, Warp, Image, Record, and the Latch/Seq system. The goal is to turn that one channel into six simultaneous channels, like a six-track mixer where each track can do everything that one channel already does.

**What we tried first, and why we stopped.** The first approach was to type out six separate copies of everything — `ampSliderA`, `ampSliderB`, `ampSliderC`, and so on, all the way through F. This hit a real wall: SuperCollider itself has a hard limit on how many separate variable names a single block of code can hold. That wasn't a mistake on your part — the language just can't hold that many names in one place, full stop. We confirmed it concretely by counting: somewhere around 300 names in, things started silently failing. So we stopped that approach, on purpose, because it genuinely can't reach all six channels.

**What we're doing instead.** Rather than six separate names, we use one array that holds six things inside itself — so instead of `ampSliderA` through `ampSliderF`, there's just `ampSliders`, where `ampSliders[0]` is channel A's fader, `ampSliders[1]` is channel B's, and so on. One name, no matter how many channels — which sidesteps the wall entirely.

**What's genuinely solid right now.** The variable declarations at the top of the file have been converted to this array style. Most of the code throughout the file has been renamed to match — a big pass, and it mostly landed correctly. Along the way we also caught and fixed a handful of small typos.

**What's actually missing — the one real gap.** One specific structural piece hasn't happened yet: a big chunk of the file — everything that builds a single channel's actual content (its mode switcher, its Grains/Loop/Warp/Image/Record/Seq modes, its slice of the master mixer) — needs to sit inside something called a loop, which is the mechanism that makes it actually run six times, once per channel. Right now that chunk has the right *names* in it, but it isn't inside that loop yet, so SuperCollider doesn't reliably know which of the six channels it's building at any given moment — which is what's been causing the errors. We've already found exactly where that chunk starts and ends (the file even has comments marking it: "Synth A view" at the start, "Synth A view END" at the end), so it's a known, bounded task, not an open mystery.

That's genuinely all of it. Nothing is in worse shape than "understood and in progress." No step attached to this — just take it in, and whenever you're ready, we can talk about what happens next.

[CLAUDE]
Good. Here's the one concrete step needed to fix the crash we found — three small edits, all part of the same idea, explained as we go.

**What we're doing:** wrapping the whole channel-building section in a loop that runs six times, so the `i` inside it always means a real channel number (0 through 5) instead of accidentally meaning nothing.

**Edit 1 — delete two redundant lines.**
Find lines 443–444:
```supercollider
subwins[i] = CompositeView.new(win, Rect(sfViewWidth+(20), 10, sfViewWidth/1.3, sfViewHeight+45))
.background=Color.yellow(0.3);
```
Delete both lines entirely. *Why:* this subwin was already built once, earlier in the file, in the first small loop. This second copy would just quietly overwrite it with a fresh, unused one — wasteful, not dangerous, but worth clearing out so there's only one subwin per channel, not two.

**Edit 2 — open the loop.**
Right after that (where those two deleted lines used to be, just before `grainViewA = CompositeView(subwins[i], ...)` — the next real line of code), add:
```supercollider
6.do({|i|
```
*Why:* this is what actually creates a valid, real `i` — a number that counts 0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 — for everything inside it to use. Without this, as we found, `i` silently means nothing at all rather than a channel number.

**Edit 3 — close the loop.**
Find line 1795, which currently reads:
```supercollider
});
```
Directly after it, add one more closing line:
```supercollider
});
```
*Why:* every `6.do({|i| ...` needs a matching closing `});` to mark where its contents end. This is that closing bracket — it marks the end of "everything that happens once per channel," right where the file's own comment says `"Synth A view END"` begins.

Once those three edits are in, everything between the new opening `6.do({|i|` and this new closing `});` — the whole type switcher, every mode, all of it — runs six times, once for each channel, with a real, working `i` each time.
