The Y position (`115`) is a fixed number that never changes across the loop's six passes — so all six channels' position-slider containers get created at the exact same spot on screen, stacked directly on top of each other. Only whichever one happens to render on top is visible or clickable; the other five are physically there, just hidden underneath.

**The fix:** make the Y position depend on `i`, the same way the soundfile view and subwin rows already do (`rowY = 10 + (i * 250)`). Change line 406 to:

```supercollider
posSliderViews[i] = CompositeView(win, Rect(10, 115 + (i * 250), sfViewWidth, 40))
```

That `115` was the right offset for a single channel sitting at the top of the window (just below its soundfile view) — adding `i * 250` shifts each subsequent channel's slider down by one full row, matching the same 250px spacing used everywhere else.

Type that in, re-run, and you should see six separate position sliders, one under each channel's soundfile view.

[CLAUDE]
Most of these are the same simple fix as before — but two of them (`outLevelMeterA` in the OSCdef, and `outLevelMonA`'s Synth creation) are a different, trickier case involving the OSC-based metering system. Let me look at that section before giving instructions.

[CLAUDE]
The file's grown since those line numbers were reported — let me find the actual current content by searching directly.

[CLAUDE]
Good — most of these are the simple fix again, but two of them (`outLevelMeterA` in the OSCdef, and the `outLevelMonA` Synth creation) are a genuinely different kind of problem, because this section lives *after* the per-channel loop — it's one-time setup code, not something that already runs 6 times. Let's do the easy part first.

**Part 1 — the straightforward renames.** `ampSliderA` and `outSelA` appear throughout the four synth-start blocks (Grains, Loop, Warp, Image, Latch/Seq). Same blanket find-and-replace as before, every occurrence:
- `ampSliderA` → `ampSliders[i]`
- `outSelA` → `outSels[i]`

That covers the errors at lines 1532, 1533, 1537, 1553, 1560, 1581, 1584, 1598, 1624, 1634, 1668, 1686.

**Part 2 — the trickier one.** The output level meter setup lives *after* the per-channel loop ends (it's part of the one-time SynthDef/effects setup), so it was only ever built once, for one channel, with no concept of "which channel" at all. Here's the actual mechanism, worth understanding since it's different from anything we've fixed so far:

```supercollider
SynthDef(\outLevelMonA, {|outBus=0|
    var sig = In.ar(outBus, 2);
    SendReply.kr(Impulse.kr(20), '/outAmpA',
        Amplitude.kr(sig.sum, 0.01, 0.1),
        0);
}).add;

OSCdef(\outMeterA,  {|msg| {outLevelMeterA.value = msg[3]}.defer},'/outAmpA');
```

That last `0` in `SendReply` is called the **replyID** — a tag you can attach to identify *which* synth sent this particular update, useful exactly when multiple synths report to the same OSC path. Right now every instance would send the same tag (`0`), so there'd be no way to tell channel A's level update from channel F's. The fix is to give each channel's monitor synth its own tag (its channel number), and have the OSCdef read that tag back out to know which meter to update.

**Step 1 — tag the SynthDef with a channel index:**
```supercollider
SynthDef(\outLevelMonA, {|outBus=0, chanIdx=0|
    var sig = In.ar(outBus, 2);
    SendReply.kr(Impulse.kr(20), '/outAmpA',
        Amplitude.kr(sig.sum, 0.01, 0.1),
        chanIdx);
}).add;
```

**Step 2 — read that tag in the OSCdef and route to the right meter.** When a `SendReply` message arrives, `msg[2]` is always that replyID/tag, and `msg[3]` is the actual value:
```supercollider
OSCdef(\outMeterA, {|msg| {outLevelMeters[msg[2]].value = msg[3]}.defer}, '/outAmpA');
```

**Step 3 — create six tagged monitor synths instead of one.** Find:
```supercollider
outLevelMonA = Synth(\outLevelMonA, [\outBus, 0], addAction: \addToTail);
```
Replace with:
```supercollider
6.do({|i|
    outLevelMons[i] = Synth(\outLevelMonA, [\outBus, outSels[i].value * 2, \chanIdx, i], addAction: \addToTail);
});
```
This also fixes another thing: each channel's monitor now watches *that channel's own* chosen output bus (via `outSels[i].value * 2`), instead of every channel watching bus 0 regardless of what they're actually set to.

One heads-up: the input meter (`meterSynthA` / `\inMeterA`) has the exact same "one-time setup, no channel concept" issue, and will need this identical tagged-replyID treatment. That's part of the Record-controls group we haven't gotten to yet, so we'll handle it there rather than now.

Take your time on both parts — tell me when they're in.

[CLAUDE]
This is a whole separate, different kind of leftover — six entirely separate functions (`centerPosInSecondsA` through `F`), defined once each, sitting *outside* any loop, all broken by the same implicit-argument gotcha from before (`i` resolves to `nil` there, since none of them are inside a `6.do`). [CLAUDE]
Now I have the full picture — two separate but related fixes needed. Both are about the same underlying idea: a *function* remembers whichever `i` was around when it was *defined*, not when it's later *called* — which is different from the plain "array got overwritten" bug we've fixed everywhere else, and needs a different kind of fix (an explicit parameter, not just array indexing).

**Fix 1 — `loadFileA`.** Currently redefined fresh on every one of the 6 loop passes, so only the last one (channel F) survives, and its body is permanently locked to whichever `i` was active when *that* definition ran — regardless of which channel's button actually calls it later.

Find the function (around line 693):
```supercollider
loadFileA = {|path, onDone|
    soundFiles[i] = SoundFile.new;
    soundFiles[i].openRead(path);
    buffers[i] = Buffer.readChannel(s, path, channels: [0], action:{{onDone.value}.defer});
    soundFileViews[i].soundfile_(soundFiles[i]);
    soundFileViews[i].read(0, soundFiles[i].numFrames);
    selectionSpecs[i] = ControlSpec(0, soundFiles[i].numFrames);
    soundFileViews[i].setSelection(0, selectionSpecs[i].map([0.1, 0.2]));
    soundFileViews[i].mouseUpAction.value(soundFileViews[i]);
};
```
Add a third parameter, `chanIdx`, and swap every `[i]` inside the body for `[chanIdx]`:
```supercollider
loadFileA = {|path, onDone, chanIdx|
    soundFiles[chanIdx] = SoundFile.new;
    soundFiles[chanIdx].openRead(path);
    buffers[chanIdx] = Buffer.readChannel(s, path, channels: [0], action:{{onDone.value}.defer});
    soundFileViews[chanIdx].soundfile_(soundFiles[chanIdx]);
    soundFileViews[chanIdx].read(0, soundFiles[chanIdx].numFrames);
    selectionSpecs[chanIdx] = ControlSpec(0, soundFiles[chanIdx].numFrames);
    soundFileViews[chanIdx].setSelection(0, selectionSpecs[chanIdx].map([0.1, 0.2]));
    soundFileViews[chanIdx].mouseUpAction.value(soundFileViews[chanIdx]);
};
```
Now, no matter which of the 6 definitions happens to survive, calling it correctly operates on whichever channel you explicitly tell it to — instead of being frozen to whichever channel was last built.

Then update both places that call it, passing `i` explicitly as that third argument:

[CLAUDE]
Ah — that's a genuinely different, clearer design than what's built right now, and it explains everything. On a laptop with just one stereo output, there's only ever one bus to route to, so the current meter (which watches "whatever's on the output bus you picked") will always show the same thing for every channel, since they're all forced onto that one bus. What you actually want is a proper mixing-desk-style meter — each channel showing *its own* signal, before it gets mixed into whatever shared output it's routed to.

That's a real architecture change, not a quick tweak — it touches all five SynthDefs (TGrains, PlayBuf, Warp, Image, Latch) plus the meter system. Here's the plan:

- Give each channel a dedicated, private audio bus — separate from the output-selector concept entirely — that only exists for metering.
- Every synth (whichever mode is currently playing on that channel) writes its own signal to that private bus, *in addition to* wherever it's actually being routed for output.
- The meter watches that private bus instead of the output bus — so it always reflects that one channel's own level, completely independent of where its audio is actually being sent.

This keeps the output selector working exactly as it does now (still genuinely useful once you're on a multi-output interface), while making the meters behave the traditional way you want.

Let's do this one step at a time. **Step 1 — allocate six private monitoring buses.** Find where `busReverb`/`busDelay` get created (the shared effect buses) and add nearby:

```supercollider
var monBuses;
```
to your var declarations, and where the other arrays get initialized:
```supercollider
monBuses = Array.newClear(6);
6.do({|i| monBuses[i] = Bus.audio(s, 2)});
```

[CLAUDE]
This second error dump is incredibly useful — it shows the *entire* variable state at crash time, and it reveals the real root cause. Look at this line from the dump:

```
var imageSynthModes = nil
```

`imageSynthModes` itself is `nil` — not an array at all — while every other array shows as `[nil, nil, nil, nil, nil, nil]`. This means the `imageSynthModes = Array.newClear(6);` initialization line is missing (or never ran), so when the loop tries to do `imageSynthModes[i] = PopUpMenu(...)`, it's really trying to write into `nil`, which crashes with exactly this "'put' not understood" error.

This also explains something bigger: notice how everything created *before* that point in the loop has channel 0 filled in (`grainViews`, `playBufViews`, `ampSliders`, `trigSliders`, etc. all show their first slot populated), while everything created *after* it is completely empty, even for channel 0 (`startButtons`, `tuneAmounts`, `verbSends`, all six nil). That's because this crash happens partway through building channel 0, and it kills the *entire* loop right there — channels 1 through 5 never get built at all, and even channel 0 doesn't finish. This one missing line likely explains most of what looked like scattered, confusing bugs in the last couple of tests.

Let me verify directly.

[CLAUDE]
Confirmed — it's declared but never initialized. That's the fix:

Find where your other arrays get `Array.newClear(6)` (near the other Image mode initializations) and add:
```supercollider
imageSynthModes = Array.newClear(6);
```

This is a big one to fix — since this single missing line was crashing the *entire* per-channel build partway through channel 0, everything we've been troubleshooting the last couple of rounds may well have just been downstream symptoms of this one thing, not separate bugs. Once it's in, do a full fresh test: reboot, and check that all six channels actually get built this time (six of everything, not just channel 1) before we chase anything else.

[CLAUDE]
Found it — one small overcorrection. `imageScanSpeeds` is a per-*channel* slider (one per channel, not one per pixel), so it never needed the extra `[px]` — that got added by pattern-matching from the lines just above it, which *did* need both indices.

```supercollider
var speed = imageScanSpeeds[i][px].value.linlin(0, 1, 0.5, 0.01);
```
→
```supercollider
var speed = imageScanSpeeds[i].value.linlin(0, 1, 0.5, 0.01);
```

Just remove the `[px]` — `imageScanSpeeds[i]` alone already gives you the right channel's slider.

[CLAUDE]
This is the same "code outside any loop still expects the old singular variable" pattern — the QUIT button is a single, one-time button (not per-channel), and its action still refers to the old `imageScanRoutineA`, but since that's now `imageScanRoutines` (an array), and the button's code isn't inside any loop, whatever `i` it's using there isn't a real channel number. [CLAUDE]
Confirmed. Since this button only exists once (not per-channel), it needs to loop over all six channels itself to stop any running scans:

```supercollider
quitButton=Button(masterWin, Rect(340, 10, 90, 60))
.states_([["QUIT ENGINE!", Color.white, Color.red]])
.action_({
    if(imageScanRoutines[i].notNil){imageScanRoutines[i].stop;imageScanRoutines[i]=nil};
    if(masterMeter.notNil){masterMeter.remove; masterMeter=nil};
    s.quit;
    win.close;

});
```
→
```supercollider
quitButton=Button(masterWin, Rect(340, 10, 90, 60))
.states_([["QUIT ENGINE!", Color.white, Color.red]])
.action_({
    6.do({|chan|
        if(imageScanRoutines[chan].notNil, {imageScanRoutines[chan].stop; imageScanRoutines[chan] = nil;});
    });
    if(masterMeter.notNil){masterMeter.remove; masterMeter=nil};
    s.quit;
    win.close;

});
```

I used `chan` instead of `i` for the loop variable here on purpose — since this button's code sits outside the main per-channel loop, there's no risk of shadowing anything, but using a distinct name makes it clear at a glance that this is a one-time cleanup loop, not the main per-channel construction.

[CLAUDE]
This looks like the window's own `onClose` handler — same bug pattern as the QUIT button (it also runs once, outside any loop, so it can't safely use a bare `i`). Let me check it.

[CLAUDE]
Confirmed — identical bug, same fix. This handler also runs once (when the window closes), not per-channel:

```supercollider
win.onClose = {
    if(imageScanRoutines[i].notNil,{imageScanRoutines[i].stop; imageScanRoutines[i]=nil;});
    if(masterMeter.notNil){masterMeter.remove;masterMeter = nil};// kills server meter

    if(recSynth.notNil) { recSynth.free };
    if(recBus.notNil) { recBus.free };
    if(recFile.notNil) { recFile.close; recFile = nil };
    log.("Thanks for your time. Goodbye");

    SystemClock.sched(0.1, { s.freeAll; nil});
};
```
→
```supercollider
win.onClose = {
    6.do({|chan|
        if(imageScanRoutines[chan].notNil,{imageScanRoutines[chan].stop; imageScanRoutines[chan]=nil;});
    });
    if(masterMeter.notNil){masterMeter.remove;masterMeter = nil};// kills server meter

    if(recSynth.notNil) { recSynth.free };
    if(recBus.notNil) { recBus.free };
    if(recFile.notNil) { recFile.close; recFile = nil };
    log.("Thanks for your time. Goodbye");

    SystemClock.sched(0.1, { s.freeAll; nil});
};
```

One thing worth knowing, not a bug to fix: `recSynth`, `recBus`, and `recFile` here are old, unrelated leftover variables from a much earlier recording design (before the buffer-based `recSynths[i]`/`\recBufA` system existed) — they're never actually assigned anything anymore, so those three `if` checks are harmless dead code, always false. Not touching them, just flagging so it doesn't look mysterious later.

[USER]
hey. take a look through the code again. for some reason the distortion drive in the Image synth isnt working

[CLAUDE]
Let me look at the Image synth's distortion chain and how the drive knob feeds into it.

[CLAUDE]
Found it — line 1155:

```supercollider
synthsStart[i].set(sinAmps[i], kb.value.linlin(0,1,0,5))
```

That first argument to `.set()` needs to be the *symbol* `\sinAmp` (naming which control on the running synth to update), but it's passing `sinAmps[i]` instead — a reference to the Knob *widget itself*. `.set()` has no idea what to do with a widget object as its control name, so the live update silently does nothing. This is likely a leftover slip from the renaming pass — easy to mix up `sinAmps[i]` (the array) with `\sinAmp` (the symbol) when they look so similar mid-edit.

Fix:
```supercollider
synthsStart[i].set(\sinAmp, kb.value.linlin(0,1,0,5))
```

Let me check whether the same mix-up happened to the other Image mode live-update knobs (cutoff, resonance, filter volume, tune) while I'm in here.

[CLAUDE]
Let me look at the full `sinAmp` knob's action block to see if it's missing the same safety guard the others have.

[CLAUDE]
Good — the safety guard is intact, so it really is just that one line. Confirmed fix:

```supercollider
synthsStart[i].set(sinAmps[i], kb.value.linlin(0,1,0,5))
```
→
```supercollider
synthsStart[i].set(\sinAmp, kb.value.linlin(0,1,0,5))
```

That's the only place this mistake happened — I checked the other Image mode knobs (tune, cutoff, resonance, filter volume) and they all correctly use their symbols (`\tune`, `\cut`, `\q`, `\filtVol`). Just this one line, at line 1155 (the knob is labeled "dist amount" right below it, matching what you're describing as the drive control). Fix that and the live drive knob should start working.

----- 2026-07-27 -----

[USER]
i'm just windering how easy it would be for me to add in a lag on the image synth and add in a range slider so I can adjust the amount of portamento before changing note?

[CLAUDE]
Quite easy, actually — SuperCollider has a UGen built exactly for this: `Lag.kr`. Wrap the frequency argument in it (`Lag.kr(freq, lagTime)`) inside the Sine/RGB/Saw/Square SynthDefs, and any `.set(\freq, ...)` call — which is exactly how the scan routine updates pitch as it moves across pixels — glides smoothly into the new value instead of jumping instantly. One new `lagTime` argument per SynthDef, one new slider, wired through the same per-channel array pattern we've used everywhere else.

One thing worth knowing: this only applies to the "held" synths (Sine, RGB, Saw, Square) — Pluck is triggered fresh per pixel, so each hit is its own separate note rather than a continuous tone gliding between pitches, and portamento wouldn't really apply to it the same way.

Want to give it a go?

[USER]
and also maybe a LFNoise to the rhytn of it so its not so straight

[CLAUDE]
Good instinct, but one small technical redirect: `LFNoise` is a *server-side* UGen — it lives inside a SynthDef, generating a continuously wobbling audio-rate or control-rate signal. The scan's timing, though, lives on the *language* side (`speed.wait` inside the Routine), which runs on a completely different clock and can't directly plug into a running UGen — a Routine just needs a plain number to wait for, and there's no live audio signal it can read from at that point.

The language-side equivalent — and it'll get you the exact same "not so straight" feel — is `rrand(lo, hi)`, which picks a fresh random number each time it's called. Instead of `speed.wait`, you'd do something like `rrand(speed * 0.5, speed * 1.5).wait` — so every step waits a slightly different, randomized amount instead of a perfectly even one, giving the scan a loose, human, non-metronomic quality.

Want both of these (the lag/portamento and the randomized scan timing) as one combined step, or take them one at a time?

[USER]
one at a time

[CLAUDE]
Let's start with the lag/portamento feature. It touches four SynthDefs (Sine, RGB, Saw, Square — the "held" ones), so let's do them one at a time, then wire up the GUI slider at the end. Let me check the current Sine SynthDef first.

[CLAUDE]
**Step 1 — the Sine SynthDef.**

Find line 2041 (the arg list) and add `lagTime=0`:
```supercollider
arg out=0, freq = 440, tune=0,sinAmp=0.3, cut=5000,q=1, filtVol=1,imageAmp=0.3, distortType=0, filterType=0, imagePan=0.0, gate=1, verbSend=0,delaySend=0, monBus=0, lagTime=0;
```

Then find line 2044:
```supercollider
sig = SinOsc.ar(freq+tune,0,  sinAmp);
```
Wrap the frequency expression in `Lag.kr`:
```supercollider
sig = SinOsc.ar(Lag.kr(freq+tune, lagTime),0,  sinAmp);
```

Here's the idea: `Lag.kr(input, lagTime)` watches `input` for changes, and whenever it jumps to a new value (which happens every time the scan routine does `.set(\freq, ...)`), instead of the oscillator snapping there instantly, `Lag.kr` glides smoothly toward it over `lagTime` seconds. At `lagTime=0` (the default), it behaves exactly as before — instant jumps, no change to current behavior until you actually turn the new knob up.

Tell me when that's in, and we'll do the RGB SynthDef next.

[CLAUDE]

```

```

`Lag.kr` handles a multichannel array input just fine — it creates one independent lag smoother per channel, so R, G, and B each glide on their own.

[USER]
done all of them. What about the pluck?

[CLAUDE]
Good question, and the honest answer is: it doesn't really apply to Pluck the same way, and adding `Lag.kr` there wouldn't do anything audible.

Here's why. `Lag.kr` only does something when its input *changes while the synth is running* — it watches for a jump and smooths the transition. But Pluck works completely differently from the other four: it's a **triggered** synth, meaning a brand new one gets created fresh for every single pixel (`Synth(imageSynthDefs[mode], args)`), plays its one note, and frees itself. Its `freq` argument is only ever set *once*, at the moment it's born — there's no `.set()` call happening to it afterward, and no "previous pitch" for it to glide *from* within its own short life. Wrapping its freq in `Lag.kr` would just output that same starting value immediately, since there's nothing to smooth.

This also fits the instrument itself — a Karplus-Strong plucked string is a percussive, decaying sound, not a sustained tone, so a "glide between notes" wouldn't really read as portamento the way it does on Sine/RGB/Saw/Square anyway; the string would decay away long before you'd hear a glide.

If you wanted *some* kind of "smoothing" feeling specifically for Pluck, it'd have to be a genuinely different mechanism — not a one-line SynthDef tweak like the others — since it'd mean changing how frequency gets chosen at the language level between one pixel and the next, not something inside the synth itself. Worth exploring separately if you're interested, but it's not really an extension of the same feature.

[USER]

[CLAUDE]
Good, there's clean space below the existing knob row. Let's add a slider there.

**Step 1 — declare and initialize.** Add to your var declarations:
```supercollider
var imageLagTimes;
```
and where the other arrays get `Array.newClear(6)`:
```supercollider
imageLagTimes = Array.newClear(6);
```

**Step 2 — add the slider.** Find the end of the `imageFiltVols[i]` knob's label block (around line 1210-1211) and add after it:

```supercollider
imageLagTimes[i] = Slider(imageSynthViews[i], Rect(10, 150, 300, 20))
.action_({|sl|
    if(synthsStart[i].notNil){
        synthsStart[i].set(\lagTime, sl.value.linexp(0.001, 1, 0.001, 2))
    }
})
.value_(0.001);
StaticText(imageSynthViews[i], Rect(10, 172, 100, 20))
.string_("portamento");
```

I used `linexp` here rather than `linlin` — frequency-glide times feel more natural on a logarithmic curve, since the difference between 0.01s and 0.1s is much more perceptually significant than between 1.0s and 1.1s. The range goes from a near-instant 1ms up to a slow 2-second glide. Default sits near-instant, matching the SynthDef's own default of `lagTime=0` (not exactly zero, since `linexp` can't reach a true zero, but close enough to be inaudible).

Tell me when it's in, and we'll wire this into the actual synth-start and scan-routine code next.

Now let's wire `\lagTime` through to the actual synths — both the scan routine's per-pixel args, and the initial start-case args. Let me find both spots.

Found it — good news, this simplifies things. The held synths only get `\freq`/`\freqR`/`\freqG`/`\freqB` updated live per-pixel; everything else (sinAmp, cutoff, filter volume, etc.) is set once at creation from the `args` array, and only gets updated live if the user turns that specific knob (which fires its own separate `.set()` call — exactly what we already wired into the slider's own action). So `\lagTime` just needs adding to that one `args` array, and it'll be correctly set the moment a synth is created — whether triggered (Pluck) or held (Sine/RGB/Saw/Square).

```

\lagTime, imageLagTimes[i].value.linexp(0.001, 1, 0.001, 2),
```

That completes the wiring — the slider now controls the initial lag time when a synth starts, and (via the slider's own action we set up earlier) can also be adjusted live while a held synth is playing. Give it a full test: load an image, pick Sine or RGB mode, turn the portamento slider up, and start the scan — you should hear the pitch glide between pixels instead of jumping.

[CLAUDE]
Got it — the scan timing randomization. Let's find the current `speed.wait` line in V02_5_2.scd.

[CLAUDE]
Found it. Replace line 1868:
```supercollider
speed.wait;
```
with:
```supercollider
rrand(speed * 0.5, speed * 1.5).wait;
```

This keeps the *average* timing centered on whatever the scan-speed slider is set to, but each individual step waits a randomized amount somewhere between half and one-and-a-half times that — so the scan stops feeling perfectly metronomic and gets a loose, human wobble instead.

Try that first as a fixed amount of looseness — if it feels right, we're done; if you want a slider to dial the *amount* of randomness up or down (tight/loose), that's an easy follow-on, same pattern as the portamento slider.

[CLAUDE]
Good — there's free horizontal space to the right of the scan speed slider (it only goes to about x=510, and the container is 700 wide), so a small knob fits nicely right beside it without needing to touch the tight vertical space below.

**Step 1 — declare and initialize.**
```supercollider
var imageScanWobbles;
```
```supercollider
imageScanWobbles = Array.newClear(6);
```

**Step 2 — add the knob**, right next to the scan speed slider:
```supercollider
imageScanWobbles[i] = Knob(imageControlViews[i], Rect(550, 5, 40, 40))
.value_(0);
StaticText(imageControlViews[i], Rect(550, 47, 60, 16))
.string_("wobble");
```

No `.action_` needed here — since the scan routine reads this knob's value fresh on every single step (same as it already does for `imageScanSpeeds[i]`), there's nothing that needs to happen *live* when you turn it; the next pixel step just picks up wherever the knob currently sits.

**Step 3 — use it in the timing calculation.** Find your `speed.wait;` line and the `var speed = ...` line just above the pixel loop, and change the wait itself:
```supercollider
rrand(speed * 0.5, speed * 1.5).wait;
```
→
```supercollider
var wobble = imageScanWobbles[i].value.linlin(0, 1, 0, 0.5);
rrand(speed * (1 - wobble), speed * (1 + wobble)).wait;
```

Here's the idea: `wobble` turns the knob's 0–1 range into a spread amount from 0 (no spread at all) to 0.5 (±50%, matching what we just tested as fixed). At knob-zero, `rrand(speed*1, speed*1)` always returns exactly `speed` — perfectly straight, no randomness. Turn it up and the timing gets progressively looser.

Take your time — tell me when it's in and give it a listen across the whole knob range.

[USER]
ERROR: syntax error, unexpected VAR, expecting '}'
  in interpreted text
  line 1755 char 14:

  											var wobble = imageScanWobble[i].value.linlin(0, 1, 0, 0.5);
             ^^^
  											rrand(speed*(1-wobble), speed*(1+wobble)).wait;
-----------------------------------

[CLAUDE]
Ah, this is a classic SuperCollider rule: **all `var` declarations inside a function must be grouped at the very top**, before any other code runs — you can't declare a new `var` partway through a block, even right before where you use it. That's different from many other languages, and an easy thing to trip on.

The fix: move `var wobble = ...` up to sit alongside the other `var` lines at the top of the pixel loop (`var freq = ...`, `var freqR = ...`, `var freqG = ...`, `var freqB = ...`, `var speed = ...`), rather than leaving it down near the `rrand(...).wait;` line where it's actually used.

So it should read like:
```supercollider
var freq = imageBrightnessDatas[i][px].max(0.001).linexp(0.001,1,80,4000);
var freqR = imageR_Datas[i][px].max(0.001).linexp(0.001,1,80, 4000);
var freqG = imageG_Datas[i][px].max(0.001).linexp(0.001,1,80, 4000);
var freqB = imageB_Datas[i][px].max(0.001).linexp(0.001,1,80, 4000);
var speed = imageScanSpeeds[i].value.linlin(0, 1, 0.5, 0.01);
var wobble = imageScanWobbles[i].value.linlin(0, 1, 0, 0.5);
```
— with the `wobble` declaration added to that group — and then further down, only the actual usage remains:
```supercollider
rrand(speed*(1-wobble), speed*(1+wobble)).wait;
```

Move that one line up and it should compile clean.
